Then Black can play a7-a6. Now white pawn on b6 is blocked. Black's b7 pawn is free to move.
This looks like a win for black.

Wait, check if the pawn on b5 was protecting b4?
White pawn is on b5. It attacks a6 and c6. It does NOT attack b4.
White pawn is on a4. It attacks b5. It does NOT attack b4.
So c5b4 is legal and safe.

Let's verify the king's position again.
Row 4: (a5)⭘ (b5)♙ (c5)♚
Row 5: (a4)♙ (b4)⭘ (c4)⭘
Black King on c5 moving to b4.
Correct.

Is there any better move?
What about c5b6? This attacks b5 (but b5 is protected by a4). It also coordinates with the b7 pawn.
What about a7a5?
If Black plays a7a5, then White can play a4-a3 or b5-b6.
If White plays b5-b6, Black can play b7b5... no.
The most active plan seems to be attacking the white pawns with the king.

Let's think about the end game.
Black's goal is to promote a pawn or capture all white pawns.
With the king on b4, Black is very close to capturing a4.
Once a4 is gone, White only has b5.
Then Black can easily stop b5 with the king or use the a7/b7 pawns.

Let's double check the moves again.
Legal moves: c5d6, c5b6, c5d5, c5d4, c5c4, c5b4, b7b6, a7a6, a7a5.
c5b4 seems the most forcing.

One detail: Row 4 is rank 5. Row 5 is rank 4.
So the King is on c5, and the pawn is on a4.
King moving from c5 to b4 is moving from rank 5 to rank 4. Correct.
White pawn on a4 is on rank 4. Black King on b4 is now on rank 4.
Black King on b4 attacks a4?
Wait, a king on b4 attacks a3, a4, a5, b3, b5, c3, c4, c5.
Yes, a4 is attacked.

Wait, if Black plays c5b4, can White play b5-b4?
No, the Black King is on b4.
Can White play a4-a5?
If a4-a5, then the pawn on b5 is no longer protected.
Then Black can take c5xb5 (wait, if Black played c5b4, the King is on b4, so b4xb5).
Then White has a pawn on a5, Black has pawns on a7, b7 and King on b5.
Black can then play a7-a6 and eventually win.

So c5b4 is a very strong move.
